⏺ It compares the multi-agent system with the single agent. It's the part of the
  evaluation the brief makes mandatory, as opposed to the evaluator you marked
  optional.

  Comparison runner (required):
  - Runs both systems on all 13 incidents with the same tools, budgets and
    models tier.
  - Collects the five numbers the brief names: root-cause accuracy, evidence
    grounding, tool-call count, latency, and token consumption (plus cost).
  - Prints a side-by-side table and saves the raw results.

  Something like:

  ┌──────────┬────────────────┬─────────────────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │ Incident │      Trap      │       Multi-agent       │   Single agent   │
  ├──────────┼────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │          │ coincidental   │ ✅ cert expiry, 14      │ ❌ blamed        │
  │ inc-002  │ deploy         │ tools, $0.21            │ deploy, 9 tools, │
  │          │                │                         │  $0.12           │
  ├──────────┼────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ inc-008  │ log search     │ ✅ inconclusive         │ ❌ claimed root  │
  │          │ down           │ (expected)              │ cause            │
  ├──────────┼────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ …        │                │                         │                  │
  ├──────────┼────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Totals   │                │ 11/13 · 92% grounded ·  │ 7/13 · 81% · 10  │
  │          │                │ avg 16 tools · $2.90    │ tools · $1.60    │
  └──────────┴────────────────┴─────────────────────────┴──────────────────┘

  These numbers are made up; they only show the shape of the output.

  How it scores, deterministically with no LLM:
  - Root-cause accuracy: does the top hypothesis or root cause match the answer
    key's root cause or one of its listed alternative phrasings? For inc-008,
    being inconclusive counts as correct.
  - Evidence grounding: do the report's evidence ids exist, and do they trace
    back to tool calls that actually ran?
  - Tool calls, latency, tokens and cost: already recorded on every run.
  - Small extras from the answer keys: did it fall for a listed red herring, and
    did it break a "must not" rule (for example "don't disable the WAF")?

  Evaluator (your optional item): the same comparison graded by an LLM judge
  instead of text matching. A judge model reads each report next to the answer
  key and decides things like:
  - "Is 'payments mTLS cert expired' the same root cause as 'certificate expiry
    on payments-svc'?" Text matching can miss that.
  - "Are the corrective actions sensible and well-ranked?"
  - "Did it actually rule out the red herring, or just not mention it?"

  It also covers agentevals trajectory checks and logging everything as
  LangSmith experiments.

  The catch: matching root causes by text is brittle. Models phrase things
  differently, so the runner would sometimes mark a correct answer wrong, or the
  reverse. My suggestion:
  1. Build the runner now with text matching, and flag uncertain matches as
     "needs review" instead of silently scoring them. Those you or I can
     eyeball; there are only 26 reports.
  2. Add the LLM judge later if you want root-cause accuracy and action quality
     judged more reliably. That's the optional evaluator.

  Want me to build the runner that way?

  The multi-agent run is cheaper because most of its work runs on Haiku, but it
  is 4× slower and makes 3× more tool calls. Both runs are saved in runs/.

  What went wrong in the multi-agent run:
  1. The change-history agent wasted its tool calls. It ran 8 very narrow
     get_changes queries (one per service, one per change kind), and most came
     back empty. It hit its 8-call limit, so it never searched past incidents or
     runbooks. It also never ran an unfiltered query, which is why the
     catalog-svc deploy was never seen. I think the cause is the prompt line
     "Narrow queries beat broad ones".
  2. The CART-212 log line was seen but dropped. The observability agent cited
     the WARN log with "22.4% v1 carts", but the "migration pending, CART-212"
     part didn't reach the report.
  3. The gaps list is noisy. It has 36 entries, mostly "no results returned"
     from those empty queries, plus near-duplicates written by different agents.
  4. The top hypothesis is still the planner's vague wording ("a recent
     deployment or config change…"). The specific explanation only appears in
     the root-cause field, because the reasoner never rewrote the hypothesis.
  5. Prompt caching is off (zero cache reads). The gatherer loops resend their
     growing history, so turning caching on is a cheap saving.

  Grounding works: one piece of evidence citing a made-up call id (budget) was
  dropped as designed.

  Suggested next steps:
  - A. Fix the general issues first:
    - change the gatherer prompt to "start broad on your main source, then
      narrow down";
    - have the reasoner rewrite vague hypotheses into specific ones;
    - remove duplicate and empty-result gaps from the report;
    - turn on prompt caching.

    None of these are specific to inc-001, but they come from a single run.
    CLAUDE.md says not to tune prompts against one scenario, so I'd test them on
    2–3 harder incidents (for example inc-002, inc-004 and inc-008) before
    relying on them.
  - B. Build step 6, the comparison runner, as planned. That gives us the
    numbers across all 13 incidents first, and A gets judged against those
    numbers rather than this one run.

  My recommendation is B, then A. Measure the systems before changing them, so
  the before/after difference shows up in the results instead of being a guess.
  Which do you want?

  What's built so far for step 6 (165 tests pass, ruff clean):

  - investigator/systems.py builds and runs either system on one scenario. The
    CLI and the comparison runner both use it, so they run the same code. Each
    run now also records the tool-call log, which the grounding checks need.
  - evals/scoring.py scores each run against the answer key with plain text
    rules, no LLM:
    - root cause right / needs review / wrong (an inconclusive answer is correct
      for inc-008);
    - whether the report fell for a listed red herring;
    - evidence grounding: do cited ids trace back to tool calls that actually
      ran;
    - key evidence: how much the system fetched, and how much it actually cited;
    - contributing factors mentioned, and security findings flagged;
    - tool calls, latency, tokens and cost.
  - evals/compare.py runs the sweep:
    - saves each run as soon as it finishes, so an interrupted sweep resumes
      without paying twice;
    - --rescore scores saved runs again without calling any model;
    - "needs review" cases can be settled by hand in reviews.yaml;
    - writes results.md and results.json.
  - AGENTS.md now lists the new modules and commands.

  A correction to what I said earlier: the multi-agent inc-001 report did
  mention CART-212. It's a cited fact under hypothesis #2, but it never made it
  into the summary or the actions. The scorer counts it as mentioned, and that's
  correct.

  The multi-agent system is better at evidence: more grounding, more key
  evidence, more contributing factors. It's worse at turning that evidence into
  a crisp answer, and much more expensive in calls and tokens.

  Why the multi-agent system lost the 4–6 cases. Each cause shows up in two or
  more incidents, so none of this is tuning to a single case:
  1. The root cause stays vague, even when the investigation found the answer.
     - The planner writes "menu" hypotheses ("a deploy or config change to A, B
       or C (timeouts, pools, cache TTLs…)").
     - Because each one covers several mechanisms, the reasoner marks it
       "supported" and never replaces it with the specific mechanism it found.
       If the reporter leaves the root cause empty, the report uses that vague
       hypothesis.
     - In inc-003, the summary correctly names the pdp_cache_include_reviews
       flag and the Redis eviction storm, but the root-cause field says "a
       deploy or configuration change…". inc-012 has the same pattern ("crawler,
       scraper, or hotlinker").
  2. The token budget forced two correct investigations to inconclusive
     (inc-010, inc-013). Both had the right hypothesis supported and then hit
     the 400K-token cap, and by design a forced stop is never reported as
     conclusive. The tokens pile up because the gatherer loops resend every tool
     result on each turn, with no prompt caching.
  3. Too many tool calls. The change-history agent runs many narrow queries that
     come back empty, and when the critic sends tasks back, the re-run repeats
     lookups that were already done. That explains 38 tool calls against 10.
  4. Hedging. The multi-agent system reported medium confidence in 5 incidents
     where high was expected, because the critic pushes back on anything "not
     proven".

  Caveats on the comparison:
  - The model mix is unequal. On budget, the single agent runs entirely on
    Sonnet 5.5, while the multi-agent gatherers and reporter run on Haiku. Part
    of the gap is the model, not the architecture.
  - Each incident ran once per system, and root-cause matching is by text, so
    some verdicts can be off.
  - I fixed one scorer bug after the sweep and rescored both systems the same
    way. inc-005 multi-agent named the right node but scored "wrong" because of
    how compound words were matched; it now counts as correct. Strictly, it
    found the right node but not the disk (EBS) failure underneath.
  - The 3-at-a-time execution doesn't distort latency much, because the time is
    spent waiting on the API.

  Your call on the 2 "needs review" verdicts. I'd mark both wrong, because
  neither root cause names the actual mechanism. inc-012 is your real incident,
  so you know best whether "crawler, scraper, or hotlinker" counts. Decisions go
  in evals/results/budget-v1/reviews.yaml, as inc-003/multi_agent: wrong, and
  are applied with --rescore.

  Commit step 6:
  git add AGENTS.md investigator/__main__.py investigator/baseline.py
  investigator/runner.py investigator/systems.py evals/ tests/test_evals.py
  git commit -m "Add comparison runner: deterministic scoring vs answer keys, 
  multi-agent vs baseline sweep, budget results

  Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5.5 <noreply@anthropic.com>"

  Next, step A, rewritten from this data:
  1. Make the planner write one mechanism per hypothesis. Have the reasoner add
     the specific mechanism as a new hypothesis once the evidence shows it, and
     rank it above the vague one. Make the reporter always state the specific
     root cause.
  2. Turn on prompt caching and stop gatherers from resending full tool results
     every turn. That fixes the token cap without just raising it.
  3. Have gatherers start with broad queries, and pass earlier evidence along
     with re-sent tasks so they don't repeat lookups.
  4. Re-run the budget sweep (about $3–4) and compare it with budget-v1.
  5. For the final write-up, do a balanced-preset sweep, or run the single agent
     on Haiku as a control to separate the model effect from the architecture
     effect.

  Commit step 6, then I'll start on A.

  The multi-agent system is better grounded but less accurate, and much slower

  - inc-010: it said inconclusive; the expected answer is conclusive with medium

  On 5 other scenarios it reported medium confidence where high was expected.

  Still open:

  out inconclusive. The critic does explain the 5 "medium instead of high"
  confidence results.

  Step A, waiting to start:
  1. Specific hypotheses: the planner writes one mechanism per hypothesis. When
     the evidence shows the specific mechanism, the reasoner adds it and ranks
     it above the vague one. The reporter always states the specific root cause.
     This fixes inc-003 and inc-012, whose root causes read "a deploy or config
     change to A, B or C".
  2. Token use: turn on prompt caching and stop the gatherers resending every
     tool result on each turn. This fixes the token-cap hits without raising the
     cap.
  3. Fewer wasted tool calls: gatherers start with broad queries, and tasks the
     critic sends back include the evidence already gathered so lookups aren't
     repeated.

⏺ The sweep is running in the background (24 runs, 3 at a time, roughly 15
  minutes). I'll report the v1 vs v2 numbers when it finishes.

  Changes made for step A so far (169 tests pass, lint clean):

  - Specific hypotheses:
    - The planner writes one mechanism per hypothesis and no "A, B or C"
      bundles.
    - The reasoner adds the specific mechanism as a new hypothesis once the
      evidence pins it down, and ranks it first.
    - The critic flags a category-style top hypothesis.
    - The reporter states the mechanism, not a category.
  - Confidence: the reasoner now has a high/medium/low scale. Open questions
    that can't change the conclusion no longer lower confidence, and the critic
    doesn't keep gathering over details that wouldn't change the ranking.
  - Fewer wasted calls: gatherers start with broad queries and make independent
    calls in the same turn. Each gatherer brief now lists every query the team
    has run, marked ok, no results or failed, so re-sent tasks don't repeat
    them.
  - Gaps list:
    - Empty results no longer go into the gaps list automatically. They're shown
      in that query list instead, and the agents decide whether an empty result
      means "nothing happened" or "the source is broken". This also applies to
      the baseline's report.
    - Gatherers only list gaps that matter for a hypothesis.
  - Prompt caching: on for gatherers and the baseline only. One-shot calls would
    just pay the 25% cache-write surcharge.

  The 400K token cap still counts cache reads, so caching only lowers cost and
  latency. Any drop in tokens the cap sees has to come from fewer turns and
  shorter context.

  Your budget-v1 results were never committed: evals/results/ still shows as
  untracked, so the second commit command didn't run. I'll give you commands for
  both result sets once v2 is done.

⏺ Pulling the per-role cost split and the baseline's weak spots from the v2 runs
  before answering:

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Short answers first: Haiku isn't why the multi-agent system is correct. The
  current data doesn't show it beating the baseline on accuracy, but there's a
  cheap, fair test that could. A couple of cost savings are left, and the "point
  3" scorer fix is small and free.

  Haiku: why it's there, and whether it explains the result

  Haiku is there to save money, not to make the system correct. In the budget
  preset, every role is one model tier below the balanced preset, so the three
  gatherers run on Haiku. The reasoner and critic, which decide the answer, run
  on Sonnet. The baseline runs entirely on Sonnet.

  So if anything, the multi-agent system is handicapped: its evidence gathering
  uses the weaker model. It still cites more of the answer key's key evidence:
  0.94 against the baseline's 0.83. The baseline missed 25–50% of the key
  evidence on 7 of 13 scenarios and still reached the right answer.

  The unequal model mix does muddy the comparison, though. A reviewer could say
  "Sonnet does the deciding in both systems, so of course they tie."

  Can we show multi-agent beats the baseline?

  Not on root-cause accuracy with the current data: both systems got 13 of 13.
  These scenarios are small enough that one Sonnet agent sees everything it
  needs in 8–10 tool calls. Every tool output is under about 2K characters.

  The aim also needs care. The assignment asks for a comparison, and AGENTS.md
  forbids case-specific tuning. Building scenarios so the baseline fails would
  be cherry-picking, and a reviewer would spot it. What we can do honestly is
  test the conditions multi-agent systems are supposed to be good at, state
  those tests before running them, and report whatever comes out.

  The cheapest and cleanest test: both systems on the same model. Run both on
  Haiku. Then the only difference is the architecture, and the question becomes
  "does splitting the work into specialists, a critic and an evidence gate let a
  cheap model do what a single agent can't?" That's a common argument for
  multi-agent systems, and this would test it.
  - Cost: roughly $0.40 for all 26 runs, because Haiku costs about 1/20 of
    Sonnet.
  - No code change: it needs only env overrides, MODEL_REASONING, MODEL_CRITIC,
    MODEL_REPORTER and MODEL_BASELINE set to Haiku.
  - Either result is useful: if multi-agent wins, that's a real finding about
    architecture. If it doesn't, we report that honestly.

  Advantages the data already shows (worth making the headline in the write-up):
  - Evidence coverage: key evidence cited 0.94 against 0.83.
  - Grounding: every claim is traceable to a tool call, 1.00 against 0.95. The
    baseline left some corrective actions uncited.
  - Rigor: the evidence gate forces every source to be checked before a report
    can be written.

  The trade-off is about 4× the latency. That's an "audit-grade investigation
  versus quick answer" story, and it holds up.

  Optional, later: 2–3 harder scenarios with realistic noise, meaning many
  services, many unrelated changes and many alerts. That's where one agent's
  single context and early stopping should hurt it. They would need to be built
  from real incident characteristics and committed before either system runs on
  them.

  More cost savings

  85% of the multi-agent cost is the Sonnet reasoner and critic. The reasoner's
  output alone is $0.49 of the $1.59 total, mostly because it rewrites a full
  rationale for every hypothesis in every round.

  1. Shorter reasoner output: cap each rationale at about 2 sentences, and don't
     re-explain a refuted hypothesis whose evidence hasn't changed. I estimate
     this cuts the multi-agent cost by 15–20%.
  2. Share the cache between reasoner and critic: they get the same evidence
     block, but different system prompts come first, so nothing is reused.
     Restructuring so the evidence comes first in both would save about 10%.
  3. Spend your credits carefully: no more full sweeps until the write-up.
     --rescore costs nothing, use --scenarios to run subsets, and the all-Haiku
     test is about $0.40.

  I wouldn't put the critic on Haiku, lower effort levels, or skip the critic
  when confidence is high. Those save money by weakening exactly what the
  multi-agent system has to show. Even with 1 and 2, the baseline stays cheaper,
  so cost won't become a selling point.

  What point 3 was

  The scorer checks whether the report mentions each contributing factor in the
  answer key by matching words: most of the factor's words have to appear in the
  report. If the report says the same thing in different words, it misses it.
  - In inc-011 the factor is "no migration linting… no
    lock_timeout/online-migration strategy". The report mentions "lint" and
    "lock_timeout" but words it differently, so it scored 0.
  - inc-005 has the same problem with its readiness-probe factor.

  Fixing it means making the matching more forgiving (word stems, synonyms) or
  adding manual review decisions in reviews.yaml, which the scorer already
  supports. It costs nothing, but it's low priority: it only affects the factors
  metric.

  Suggested order

  1. The all-Haiku comparison (about $0.40).
  2. Cost saving 1, the shorter reasoner output, so the final sweep is cheaper.
  3. Deliverables tomorrow.

  Should I start the Haiku run now?

  The whole sweep cost $0.54.

  Both misses were the right diagnosis followed by the wrong final call:
  - inc-006, baseline: its summary has the correct root cause, the fraud-model
    hot-reload causing the OOMKill. But it submitted that summary with no
    hypotheses, evidence or actions, so the report came out inconclusive. The
    baseline's submission format allows empty lists, and Haiku took that
    shortcut. The multi-agent system can't do this, because its evidence gate
    won't allow a report until the hypotheses have evidence. That's a real
    reliability difference, but it partly comes from a check the baseline
    doesn't have. If we claimed it as an architecture win, a reviewer could
    fairly answer "just validate the baseline's submission".
  - inc-013, multi-agent: it ranked the correct cause first at medium
    confidence. Then the Haiku critic decided only a human could settle it. In
    eval mode there's no human to ask, so the run stopped as inconclusive.

  What the two sweeps show together:
  - Accuracy: no difference on this dataset, with either model.
  - Multi-agent advantages: fully grounded claims every time, and the structural
    guarantees (every source checked, a structured report always produced).
  - Multi-agent costs: 2–4× the latency, 2–3× the cost, and on Haiku it hedges,
    reporting medium where high was correct in 8 scenarios.

  So I can't prove the multi-agent system wins, and I'd advise against building
  scenarios until it does. A clearly written negative or mixed result, with the
  trade-offs measured, is a stronger submission than a rigged win. The honest
  claim is: "same accuracy, stronger auditability guarantees, at higher cost and
  latency".

  Options before tomorrow:
  1. Close the format loophole in the baseline (about $0.01, my recommendation):
     require at least one hypothesis when the outcome is conclusive, then
     re-run just the inc-006 baseline. That keeps the comparison from being won
     on a technicality.
  2. Harder, noise-heavy scenarios (about $1–2): this is where a single agent's
     one context and early stopping should hurt. They should be designed from
     real incident characteristics and committed before either system runs, with
     results reported whatever they show.
  3. Stop here and write it up as is.

  once you've chosen.

⏺ The baseline loophole is fixed, and the inc-006 Haiku baseline is now correct.
  On all-Haiku the baseline now gets 13 of 13 against the multi-agent system's
  12 of 13.

  The fix: the baseline's submission schema now requires at least one
  hypothesis. A conclusive report also needs its rank-1 hypothesis marked
  supported, with at least one supporting fact that cites tool calls. That's the
  same bar the multi-agent evidence gate enforces. A submission that fails goes
  back to the model to correct, as other format errors already do. Two new
  tests cover it, and all 171 tests pass.

  The re-run of inc-006 cost $0.007 and the baseline submitted a complete,
  conclusive report. The saved run doesn't record whether the new check had to
  send a submission back first. The old run is kept at
  $CLAUDE_JOB_DIR/tmp/inc-006-baseline.before-fix.json.

  Updated all-Haiku results: baseline 13/13 against multi-agent 12/13. The
  baseline is also about 2× faster and about 3× cheaper. The multi-agent
  system's only remaining edges are grounding (1.00 against 0.96) and key
  evidence cited.

  Would GPT o-series reasoning models help prove multi-agent is better? I don't
  think so. The limit is the dataset, not the model. The baseline already scores
  13/13 on Sonnet and on Haiku, so no model can produce a visible gap. A
  stronger reasoning model also helps the single agent at least as much, because
  it plans, cross-checks and second-guesses itself inside one loop. That's
  exactly the work the multi-agent system splits across agents.

  Giving only the multi-agent system o-series models would be an unfair
  comparison. The fair version, the same model in both systems, is what we just
  tested with Haiku, and it tied.

  There's one model-related test that is legitimate: mixing model families,
  which only the multi-agent design can do. For example, Claude gatherers and
  reasoner with a GPT critic, so the critic's blind spots don't match the
  reasoner's. The code already supports this (MODEL_CRITIC=openai:<model>, plus
  langchain-openai and an OPENAI_API_KEY). But it would still hit the same
  ceiling on these 13 scenarios. It only becomes worth paying for alongside
  harder scenarios, and you said credits are short, so I'd mention it in the
  technical note as a possible improvement rather than run it.

  Commit for the fix (code and tests only; no eval runs, as you asked, and I've
  saved that as a preference):

  git add investigator/baseline.py tests/test_baseline.py
  git commit -m "Baseline: reject conclusive submissions without a supported, 
  evidenced top hypothesis

  Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5.5 <noreply@anthropic.com>"

  evals/results/ will keep showing as untracked. If you'd like it hidden, I can
  add it to .gitignore. When you commit your chosen run tomorrow, use git add 
  -f: the per-run JSONs sit under runs/, which the existing runs/ rule already
  ignores.

⏺ I framed that badly. The fix was the option you picked, but I should have put
  it as "the loophole is closed, and here is what's still costing the
  multi-agent system", not as a baseline win. Here's that diagnosis, and a plan
  for a test the multi-agent system can legitimately win.

  Why the multi-agent system lost inc-013 (all-Haiku run)

  It found the right cause and ranked it first. Then the critic answered "needs
  a human". In eval mode no human is available, and the orchestrator turns that
  answer straight into an inconclusive report.

  That's a design flaw in our orchestration, not a weakness of multi-agent
  systems in general. The critic is offered an option that can never work in
  eval mode. The fix is general: in eval mode, tell the critic no human is
  available, so it must choose between accepting (listing its open questions in
  the report) and inconclusive. inc-008, where inconclusive is the right answer,
  keeps that path.

  The multi-agent system's other weak spot is hedging. On Haiku it reported
  medium confidence where high was right in 8 scenarios. The critic and the
  evidence gate make it cautious.

  Why the baseline isn't failing

  The scenarios are too easy for any test to separate the two systems. But the
  runs show a consistent weakness in the baseline. Across the 13 Sonnet runs, it
  never called these tools in this many runs:

  ┌───────────────────────┬───────────────────┬─────────────┐
  │         tool          │ baseline (Sonnet) │ multi-agent │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ get_traces            │ 10/13             │ 0/13        │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ search_past_incidents │ 9/13              │ 1/13        │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ get_infra_events      │ 5/13              │ 0/13        │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ get_external_report   │ 5/13              │ 2/13        │
  └───────────────────────┴───────────────────┴─────────────┘

  The single agent stops as soon as it has a convincing story, usually after
  about 8 tool calls. This is called premature closure, and it's a well-known
  failure in real root-cause analysis. The multi-agent system is built against
  it: the evidence gate forces every specialist to run, and the critic can send
  work back.

  In the current scenarios, that early story happens to be correct. So the
  weakness exists but never costs the baseline anything.

  Proposed test: 2–3 "premature closure" scenarios

  Each scenario has a decoy that looks complete in the sources a single agent
  checks first: metrics, logs and the change history around the incident. The
  evidence that rules the decoy out sits in a source the baseline skipped, for
  example:
  1. Traces: the decoy deploy's service looks guilty in logs and metrics, but
     the traces show the latency or error comes from a downstream call.
  2. Infra events: errors line up with a deploy, but a node, disk or network
     event that started first explains them. The deploy was only rolled out onto
     the affected nodes.
  3. Two causes at once: a real but minor cause the agent finds early, and a
     bigger second cause that only appears when the agent keeps going.

  To keep this credible to a reviewer:
  - Generic stories with fictional organisations, written to the same standard
    as the other scenarios.
  - No prompt changes for these cases, and the same tools and budgets for both
    systems.
  - Each scenario is written once and run once per system, and we report
    whatever happens. If the baseline still gets one right, that stays in the
    results. Rewriting a scenario until the baseline fails would be rigging, and
    a reviewer can tell.
  - The technical note states the design openly: these scenarios target
    premature closure, a failure mode measured in our own runs, not a gap
    invented for the test.

  Cost: about $0.01–0.05 per scenario for both systems on Haiku, and about
  $0.15–0.30 on the budget preset. With the critic fix, re-checking all 16
  scenarios on Haiku is about $0.60.

  Order of work, if you approve

  1. The eval-mode critic fix, plus tests. Free.
  2. Write inc-014 to inc-016 with answer keys, then run the validator and the
     tests. Free.
  3. Run all 16 scenarios on all-Haiku, then only the new scenarios on the
     budget preset. About $1.20 in total.

  I can't guarantee the baseline fails the new scenarios. A strong model
  sometimes checks traces anyway. But these target a weakness we measured, so
  the multi-agent system has a real chance to show its advantage on fair terms.
  Go ahead with this plan?

⏺ The all-Haiku sweep is done too. The new scenarios didn't separate the two
  systems: the baseline solved all three on both models.

  All-Haiku, 16 scenarios (with the critic fix and the baseline fix):

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────┬──────────────┐
  │                                     │  multi-agent  │   baseline   │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Root-cause accuracy                 │ 14/16         │ 16/16        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ New scenarios (inc-014 to inc-016)  │ 3/3           │ 3/3          │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Key evidence fetched / cited        │ 0.97 / 0.97   │ 0.91 / 0.90  │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Claims citing real tool calls       │ 1.00          │ 0.97         │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Latency per run / cost, all 16 runs │ 131 s / $0.52 │ 58 s / $0.17 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────┴──────────────┘

  Budget preset, new scenarios only: both systems 3/3. The multi-agent system
  cited all the key evidence in each, against the baseline's 0.67 on inc-014 and
  inc-016.

  What went wrong with the premature-closure idea: the baseline didn't close
  early here. The topology notes ("automatic failover enabled", the CoreDNS
  replica count) and the reporter's questions sent it to get_infra_events within
  its first 4–9 calls. On the budget preset its answers are correct and
  specific: the cache failover, the evicted CoreDNS replicas, the backfill job.
  It wasn't lucky text matching.

  Why the multi-agent system lost inc-003 and inc-005 (all-Haiku): in both, the
  right answer was ranked first, and the run still ended inconclusive at the
  final step.
  - inc-003: the Haiku reasoner marked a second hypothesis, a slow catalog-db,
    as equally supported. The critic then called it inconclusive.
  - inc-005: the run hit the 400K token cap. Haiku writes much longer outputs,
    and a forced stop is never reported as conclusive.

  Every multi-agent miss across all the runs follows this pattern. Its
  investigation is the stronger one; it loses at the final decision, where a
  cautious critic or the budget overrides a correct top hypothesis. The baseline
  never fails that way because nothing second-guesses it.

  Where this leaves the comparison. Across every sweep, the multi-agent system:
  - is better on evidence: more of the key evidence fetched and cited, every
    claim grounded, and every source checked;
  - is no better on accuracy: on par or slightly behind, depending on the model;
  - costs 2–4× in latency and cost.

  The dataset can't show an accuracy win, and I'd stop adding scenarios to chase
  one. That would be iterating on the test until the result comes out, which a
  reviewer would see straight through. Also, each sweep is a single run per
  scenario, so a one- or two-scenario difference is within noise.

  What I'd do instead:
  1. Fix the decision step, the multi-agent's one real weakness, in a general 
     way. When the critic answers inconclusive, it must name either the
     competing hypothesis that is equally supported or the missing source that
     blocks a decision. If it can't, it has to accept. That costs nothing to
     build. A Haiku re-run to check it is about $0.70.
  2. Make evidence quality the headline of the write-up. It's the multi-agent
     system's measured advantage, and it's what matters in a real postmortem:
     every claim traceable to a tool call, every source checked. Present
     accuracy as equal and cost as the trade-off.
  3. Include the inc-006 story. Without a gate, the Haiku baseline once
     submitted an empty report. The fix we added to the baseline is a small copy
     of the multi-agent system's evidence gate.

  Total spend this session is about $4.30. Should I do 1, or stop here and leave
  the rest for tomorrow's write-up? Nothing is committed yet: the critic fix,
  the baseline fix, the 3 new scenarios and the doc updates are all in the
  working tree.

           anual action). Ask precise questions.

  What changed since the last commit:
  - Critic in eval mode: it's told no human is available, so it can't end a run
    on "needs a human".
  - Justified inconclusive: an inconclusive verdict must name a blocking gap or

    2–3 scenarios flip between identical runs, and every miss is a correct top

    evidence: key evidence cited 0.94 vs 0.83, and every claim grounded (1.00 vs

❯ my idea for this is, smart models will do their best whether in baseline mode 
  or in multi agwnt mode, so it becomes model agnostic, but a freakin good      
  arch is resilient, along with limitations and hallucinations or stupidity of  
  small models (or combinations of small models with medium models at some      
  nodes). so it helps us achieve wonders with small privileges. a heavy opus or 
  fable level model can solve my problem i know that already. what do you       
  think                                                                         

⏺ Your thesis is sound, and it's the strongest framing this project has. Strong
  models do well in either setup, so the architecture is what has to make small
  models dependable. Tonight's data supports it in part and pushes back in one
  important place.

  Where the data supports you:
  - Small models work well as gatherers. With Haiku gathering, the multi-agent
    system cited 0.95–0.97 of the key evidence, against 0.90 for the Haiku
    baseline. Narrow, bounded tasks play to a small model's strengths.
  - Structure catches small-model mistakes. The Haiku baseline once submitted an
    empty report, and only a gate fixed that. The evidence gate and the
    grounding checks are enforced in code, so the model can't skip them.
  - The mixed setup is your thesis in practice: Haiku gatherers with a Sonnet
    judge. It matched the Sonnet baseline across all 16 scenarios, with better
    evidence coverage.

  Where it pushes back: every multi-agent miss tonight happened at an LLM
  judgment step: a Haiku critic calling inconclusive, or wrongly accepting.
  Adding agents also adds decision points, and each one is a new place a small
  model can be wrong. An architecture is only as resilient as its weakest LLM
  decision. So the resilience has to come from deterministic structure, not from
  adding more LLM opinions. Our critic is currently a weak point, not a
  safeguard.

  Two changes would make the architecture match your thesis:

  1. Move the final decision out of the LLM. The critic stays as the reviewer
     that requests more evidence, but whether a run ends conclusive or
     inconclusive is decided by fixed rules over the evidence. A sketch of those
     rules:
     - the top hypothesis is supported by at least 2 independent sources;
     - no contradicting evidence against it is left unaddressed;
     - no key source failed.

     A small model can't then talk itself out of a correct answer or into a
     wrong one. Every miss tonight would have been decided by these rules
     instead.
  2. Test the thesis under stress, not on easy scenarios. On this dataset Haiku
     alone already scores 16/16, so there's no gap for the architecture to
     close. That shows the scenarios are too easy, not that the thesis is wrong.
     Instead, weaken the models step by step: Haiku at medium effort, then low.
     Compare the single agent against the mixed multi-agent setup as they get
     weaker, ideally with a few runs per scenario. If the single agent's
     accuracy drops faster than the multi-agent system's, that curve is your
     proof. Testing at low effort is cheap: about $0.30 for the baseline and
     about $1.50 for the multi-agent system.

  The pitch for the technical note would then be: "Architecture is what lets
  small models hold up as they weaken: cheap models gather, code enforces
  grounding and the evidence gate, and the expensive judgment is made once, by a
  capable model or by fixed rules."

  My suggestion for tomorrow: build change 1 first, then run the stress test,
  then commit whichever run backs that claim. Whatever it shows goes into the
  write-up as measured.

  Recalled 1 memory, wrote 2 memories (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I've saved your thesis and tonight's results in my project notes, so tomorrow
  starts from here.

  suggestion to make the final decision with fixed rules: the Critic has to be

  Checking our design against Section A: all six roles exist. Agents exchange
  structured Outcomes, and the critic sends work back to the gatherers. One
  mismatch could draw a reviewer comment:
  - The brief's Dependency Agent covers deployments, configuration changes and
    service dependencies.
  - We split that between dependency (topology) and change_history (deploys and
    config).

  "At least five" allows the extra agent, but we should either merge them or
  explain the split in the write-up. That's tomorrow's decision.

  On cheaper models: I agree it's worth trying, and it also tests your

  o3 for reasoning. Per-role MODEL_<ROLE> overrides still work across

⏺ The o3 baseline is a telling result:

  scenario: inc-003
  stop reason: accepted
  tool calls: 21
  tokens: 170K
  outcome: ✅ correct
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  scenario: inc-008
  stop reason: token cap
  tool calls: 38
  tokens: 434K
  outcome: inconclusive. That's scored correct, but only because the budget ran
  out, not because it judged the evidence insufficient.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  scenario: inc-016
  stop reason: token cap
  tool calls: 38
  tokens: 435K
  outcome: ❌ It found "database IOPS saturation" but never found the backfill
  job, then ran out.

  Run alone, o3 makes one tool call per turn, so every tool result piles into a
  single growing context, until the 400K budget cuts it off. That's the failure
  mode the multi-agent design exists to prevent: each specialist works in its
  own short context, and the orchestrator merges structured results. Claude
  models didn't hit this as a single agent; o3 does.

  I'll report the comparison once the multi-agent re-run finishes.

  Per scenario:
  - inc-016 (premature closure): this is the scenario built for this test, and
    it separated the systems.
    - The multi-agent system found the backfill job and named the pool cut as a
      contributing factor.
    - The single o3 agent got as far as "database IOPS saturation", then hit the
      400K token cap after 38 tool calls, one per turn, and never found the
      job.
  - inc-003: both correct.
  - inc-008: both reports came out inconclusive, which is the expected answer.
    But neither system decided that from the evidence: both were stopped by a
    budget limit. The multi-agent system hit the tool-call cap with a specific
    theory ranked first, and the baseline hit the token cap. Both count as
    correct, but the write-up has to say how.

  This supports your thesis. A single model piles every tool result into one
  context until it runs out. The multi-agent system keeps each specialist's
  context short and merges structured results, so it used fewer tokens and still
  finished. The cost is higher, though: gpt-5.4 output is expensive ($15 per 1M
  tokens), so the multi-agent system cost 2.3× more.

  Caveats:
  - Only 3 scenarios, one run each.
  - The baseline runs came from the first attempt, which used OpenAI's
    chat-completions API. The multi-agent runs used the Responses API. It's the
    same model and effort, but not perfectly matched. Re-running the baseline on
    the Responses API would cost about $1.

  About the bug: my first OpenAI run was broken because gpt-5.4 rejects tool
  calls with a reasoning effort on the chat-completions endpoint. I had tested
  structured output but not the tool loop. I switched to the Responses API,
  tested a real tool loop on both models, and re-ran only the multi-agent side;
  the invalid runs are set aside in the scratchpad. OpenAI spend tonight is
  about $3.75, including the about $0.39 wasted on the broken run.

  Provider switch: set INVESTIGATOR_PROVIDER=openai in .env, or pass --provider 
  openai to either CLI. Studio has a provider field too. All 196 tests pass.

⏺ Both systems reached nearly the same conclusion, and both missed the same
  thing. Here's what each said, then the facts in your data that neither one
  retrieved.

  Multi-agent (gpt-5.4 gatherers, o3 reasoner and critic)

  Run: conclusive, high confidence. 1 round, 32 tool calls, 182 s, $0.48.

  - Planner: proposed 4 hypotheses, two of them taken from the reporter: the
    v3.14.0 deploy made queries more expensive; sale traffic saturated
    catalog-db; an api-gateway timeout or limit on the search route; contention
    inside catalog-db. It sent out 4 tasks.
  - Gatherers: produced 25 evidence items. Observability had 12, Dependency 7,
    Change history 6. The key findings:
    - everything inflects at 14:10, not at the 14:05 deploy;
    - search-svc pool wait went from 3 ms to 2.9 s while database query
      execution time stayed flat (4.7–4.9 ms);
    - the failing trace shows 4 gateway attempts × 1500 ms;
    - the HPA scaled search-svc from 6 to 10 pods at 14:22;
    - the database reached 500/500 connections and logged "too many
      connections".
  - Reasoner: added its own top hypothesis: database connection exhaustion after
    the sale surge, made worse by the HPA scale-up (10 pods × 50 connections
    = 500). It refuted the deploy theory and the database-contention theory.
    Sale saturation and the gateway timeout stayed "supported, medium".
  - Critic: accepted, while flagging 4 issues. One was: "Gateway retry fan-out 
    (640 rps → 2,500 rps upstream attempts) is a significant load amplifier but 
    is treated only as a symptom; the RCA might call this out more explicitly as
    a contributing factor."
  - Report: root cause is connection exhaustion during the sale surge, worsened
    by the HPA scale-out, with gateway retries amplifying load. Actions, in
    order: limit gateway retries and rate-limit search; cap HPA replicas or the
    pool size; add a connection budget or pooler; capacity-test the full path;
    add alerts on pool wait and retry amplification.

  Baseline (o3 alone)

  Run: conclusive, high confidence. 16 tool calls, 60 s, $0.14.

  - Root cause: "catalog-db exhausted max_connections (500) and CPU under the 
    sale-induced load… The API-gateway's 4× retry policy amplified the load."
  - Hypotheses: connection exhaustion (supported, high); deploy regression
    (refuted); retries alone overloaded the system (left open).
  - Actions: scale up catalog-db and raise max_connections to 1,500; cut the
    pool to 20 or add PgBouncer; lower gateway retries to 1 with backoff; add
    alerts.

  What neither system looked at

  Both queried the change history for search-svc only, so neither found this
  record:

  ▎ 13:58 api-gateway config: route /search timeout 5000 ms → 1500 ms, retries 1
  ▎ → 3, retry on timeout and 5xx, no backoff

  The matching config reload log line at 13:58:10 was also never retrieved: the
  multi-agent system searched gateway logs at ERROR level only, and the baseline
  searched only WARN and INFO for specific words.

  Neither system opened the capacity_test_2026_q1 report either. It says the old
  gateway policy handled 2.5× traffic, and that catalog-db saturates at about
  1,100 queries per second. The multi-agent system asked for a report named
  "config snapshots", which doesn't exist, and didn't list the available ones.

  Both noticed that retries were multiplying load, but treated them as a symptom
  with no trigger, because they never saw the change that introduced them. I'll
  leave the verdict to you.

  Lessons for our system, not specific to this case:
  1. gpt-5.4 ignored the "start broad" instruction: the prompt says one
     unfiltered change-history query covers every service, and it filtered by
     service anyway.
  2. The critic accepted despite flagging an unexplained amplifier. That's the
     kind of gap it should have sent back as a request for more evidence.

  Everything is in $CLAUDE_JOB_DIR/tmp/adhoc/:
  - case.yaml: your case, with the 4 timeout span statuses converted to error
    plus an error: timeout attribute so they fit our schema;
  - multi_agent.json and baseline.json: each system's full output, including the
    planner's plan, all evidence and the critic's verdicts.

  Nothing went into dataset/ or evals/.

      statement: The 13:58 api-gateway policy change for /search (upstream      
  timeout cut from 5000ms to 1500ms, retries raised from 1 to 3, no backoff)    
  was latent under normal load. When the 14:10 sale email lifted traffic to     
  about 1.6x, a small tail of requests exceeded 1.5s and was retried            
  immediately. Each client request became up to 4 upstream attempts (about 650  
  client rps turned into about 2500 attempts per second), far above             
  catalog-db's roughly 1100 qps capacity. Because search-svc does not cancel    
  queries when the client disconnects, abandoned queries kept holding DB        
  connections, pool wait grew to about 2.9s, and every attempt timed out, which 
  produced more retries. The overload is a self-sustaining retry storm.         
      fault_type: retry_amplification_overload                                  
      entity: api-gateway route /search upstream timeout and retry policy

  connections and CPU.

  pool acquisition and only for connected clients, so it hides the 2.9s pool

  healthy while the gateway sees timeouts.                                      
      must_mention: true

  retries on timeout, which multiplies load exactly when the backend is slow.

  es were flat query time (4.7 to 4.9 ms) and failures starting at 14:10 rather 
  than at the 14:05 deploy.                                                     
                                                                                
  **Corrective actions (7 vs 4 of 10):** The multi-agent plan matches the key's 
  ranks 1, 2, 5 and 6 reasonably well, though it limits retries rather than     
  reverting the policy. It lacks backoff and jitter, query cancellation, and a  
  gate on gateway config changes. The baseline's first action is to scale up    
  the DB and raise `max_connections` to 1,500, which is on the `must_not` list  
  and probably harmful (see below). It also lowers retries and adds alerts.     
                                                                                
  **Calibration (2 vs 1 of 5):** Both said high confidence. That matches the    
  key's expected confidence, but it is overconfident for a root cause that      
  skipped the trigger. The baseline also left "retries alone overloaded the     
  system" open while still claiming high confidence.                            
                                                                                
  ## Why both failed the same way                                               
                                                                                
  Both runs picked the loudest, latest symptom as the cause. The "too many      
  connections (500/500)" log is the most dramatic line, but the metrics show it 
  can't be the cause:                                                           
  - At 14:15 the 504 rate was already 52%, and at 14:20 it was 88%.             
  - Database connections were only 300/500 at both of those times, which is 6   
  pods times 50.                                                                
  - Connections hit 500 only after the 14:22 HPA scale-up, so the exhaustion    
  followed the failure.                                                         
                                                                                
  The database is also CPU-bound at about 1,100 qps. Raising `max_connections`  
  to 1,500 would add no capacity and would let more concurrent queries pile     
  onto a saturated DB.                                                          
                                                                                
  The multi-agent planner also took two of its four hypotheses straight from    
  the reporter (the deploy and sale saturation), which probably anchored the    
  rest of the run.                                                              
                                                                                
  ## Cost versus quality                                                        
                                                                                
  The multi-agent run scored about 22 points higher for roughly 3.4x the cost   
  and 3x the latency. That is one run each, so it is a weak signal. Neither run 
  is useful on its own, because both would have steered an engineer toward the  
  wrong fix first.                                                              
                                                                                
  ## Suggested answer key update                                                
                                                                                
  Both agents fell for the same distractor, so I'd add it as an explicit red    
  herring:                                                                      
                                                                                
  ```yaml                                                                       
  - claim: catalog-db max_connections exhaustion is the root cause.             
    why_wrong: 504s were already 52% at 14:15 while connections were 300/500.   
  Pool saturation is app-side (50 per pod). The 500/500 state came after the    
  14:22 HPA scale-up. The DB is CPU-bound at about 1100 qps, so more            
  connections add no capacity.                                                  
    disproved_by: [ev_db_metrics, ev_gateway_metrics, ev_hpa]
  ```                                                                           

  `must_not`.

⏺ Short version: I agree with the outside review's main verdict, with two
  corrections. But the bigger finding is that our own scorer graded this case
  backwards.

  Our scorer got it wrong

  ┌─────────────────┬─────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┐
  │                 │       our scorer        │          reality           │
  ├─────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │                 │ ✅ correct (alias       │ ❌ It blames database      │
  │ Baseline root   │ "retry amplification",  │ connection exhaustion;     │
  │ cause           │ match 1.0)              │ retries appear only as an  │
  │                 │                         │ amplifier.                 │
  ├─────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Multi-agent     │ 🟡 review (0.57)        │ ❌ Same wrong cause,       │
  │ root cause      │                         │ though a closer call.      │
  ├─────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Required key    │                         │ Neither fetched the 13:58  │
  │ evidence        │ 1.0 for both            │ gateway change.            │
  │ fetched         │                         │                            │
  ├─────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Must-mention    │ multi-agent 3/3         │ It never actually stated   │
  │ factors         │                         │ those factors.             │
  └─────────────────┴─────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┘

  There are three causes, and none is specific to this case:
  1. Root-cause matching only checks words. The baseline's text contains "retry
     policy amplified the load", which matches the alias "retry amplification"
     even though retries are described as a side effect. The matcher can't tell
     a cause from a contributing factor.
  2. Key evidence counts as fetched if the right tool was called with matching 
     arguments. It doesn't check what came back. Both systems queried changes
     filtered to search-svc, which can't return the gateway change, yet
     ev_changes counts as fetched. The same applies to the ERROR-level log
     search and the config reload INFO line.
  3. Factor mentions are word overlap. The multi-agent report uses "cancel",
     "handler latency" and "pool wait" heavily, so it gets credit without ever
     stating the factors.

  So the earlier sweep numbers may include false positives too, especially for
  key evidence and factors. I hand-checked many of the root-cause verdicts
  earlier, but not all of them. This has to be fixed before you commit an eval.

  The outside review: mostly right

  Confirmed against the raw outputs:
  - The missed fact was the 13:58 gateway policy change. Neither system saw it
    because both filtered the change history to search-svc. Neither opened
    capacity_test_2026_q1 either.
  - The baseline broke a must_not: its first action is "scale catalog-db to a
    larger instance class and raise max_connections to 1,500". The multi-agent
    system broke none.
  - The timeline point is right: 504s were at 52% at 14:15 while connections
    were 300/500, and they reached 500 only after the 14:22 HPA scale-up.
    Connection exhaustion followed the failure.
  - Cost: 3.4× the cost, 3× the latency.

  Two corrections:
  - Cancelled queries: "Neither caught that search-svc doesn't cancel abandoned
    queries" is half right for the multi-agent system. It recorded that the
    search spans "ran to completion despite cancellation" and that cancellations
    hit 2,200 per second, but never turned that into a contributing factor.
  - The multi-agent critic actually spotted the flaw. Its first issue was
    "Pool-wait spikes begin at 14:10 when catalog-db has only ~210 active 
    connections… per-pod pool saturation precedes database-level exhaustion."
    That's the same argument the review uses to rule out connection exhaustion.
    Then it accepted anyway. The architecture produced the right doubt, and the
    critic didn't act on it.

  The review's suggested answer-key addition (connection exhaustion as an
  explicit red herring, plus "raise max_connections" in must_not) is sound. It's
  your key, so that's your call.

  What I'd fix, all general

  1. Scorer: save tool results with each run, so key evidence counts only when
     the returned data actually contains it. Add a model-based grader for the
     root cause that uses the answer key and checks cause against amplifier
     against trigger, ideally running on a different model family. Keep the word
     matching as a cheap first pass. Saved runs can be re-scored for cents,
     with no investigation re-runs.
  2. Critic: if one of its own issues contradicts the top hypothesis's causal
     order (the effect appears before the proposed cause), it must ask for more
     evidence instead of accepting.
  3. Change-history sweep: gpt-5.4 ignored the "start broad" instruction. The
     orchestrator could make the first change-history query in each
     investigation unfiltered (all services), so no model can skip it. This is a
     structural guarantee, not a judgment the critic makes.

  Nothing was added to dataset/ or evals/. The key and the scoring script are in
  $CLAUDE_JOB_DIR/tmp/adhoc/ with the runs. For the scorer, I converted the
  key's tags and its get_trace evidence tool to names our schema accepts; that
  changed nothing else.

  These three fixes are worth doing before tomorrow's committed eval run,
  starting with the scorer, since every comparison depends on it. Should I plan
  them out?

       9  
      10 +`key_evidence[].must_contain` lists strings the returned tool d
         +ata must hold for a call to count as retrieving that evidence, 
         +so a filtered query that could not return it doesn't count. `at
         +tempt_only` marks evidence that is the attempt itself (trying a
         + source that fails). The validator checks every `must_contain` 
         +string occurs in the scenario, and a test checks the key's own 
         +arguments can return it.                                       
      11 +                                                               
      12  Scenario ids are neutral (`inc-NNN`) so ids and filenames don't
           hint at the answer. `tool_faults` are applied by the tool harn
          ess and never returned to the agent. The answer-key format foll
          ows the four-layer ground truth in Alibaba's RCA Benchmark: fau
          lt type, entity, propagation chain, and evidence. `key_evidence
          [].tool` and `args` map to agentevals' superset trajectory matc
          h.

⏺ All three fixes are in, and 219 tests pass. On your case the fixed multi-agent
  system now gets the right root cause, and the judge confirms it; the baseline
  is still graded wrong. That's the case the fixes came from, though, so it
  doesn't show they generalize.

  1. Scorer:
  - Tool data saved: each run now records what every tool call returned.
  - Key evidence checked against that data: 32 required items now carry
    must_contain strings, so a filtered query that couldn't return the evidence
    no longer counts. query_metrics items are still checked by tool and
    arguments only.
  - No ground truth changed: only matching fields were added.
  - Checks on the strings: the validator confirms each one exists in its
    scenario, and a test confirms the key's own arguments can return it.
  - inc-008 fix: its "attempts the log search" item can now be satisfied
    (attempt_only). Before, it could never count for either system.
  - LLM judge (evals/judge.py, JUDGE_MODEL, default gpt-5.4-mini): grades
    whether the report's root cause is the key's cause or only an amplifier or
    trigger, plus red herrings, must_not items and factors. It costs about half
    a cent per run, results are cached in <dir>/judgements/, and --judge none
    turns it off. If the judge's provider has no API key, the run falls back to
    lexical scoring with a warning.

  2. Critic: it may no longer accept while one of its own issues undermines the
  top hypothesis's causal chain, for example when the effect appears before the
  proposed cause or a significant observation is left unexplained.

  3. Change history: the agent's code now always runs one unfiltered change
  query covering every service, from 14 days before the incident, before the
  model starts. A model can't skip it.

  On your case ($CLAUDE_JOB_DIR/tmp/adhoc2/; the run cost $0.58 and took 195 s):
  - The sweep found the 13:58 gateway change, and the report now names it as the
    root cause, with the sale traffic as the trigger.
  - Its first action is to revert the /search policy, and it adds backoff,
    jitter and a retry budget.
  - The judge graded it correct, with no red herrings and no must_not
    violations. 0.8 of the key evidence was retrieved and cited.
  - The critic still accepted after one round. The sweep made the difference
    this time, not the critic change.

  Your openai-3 results also change under the new scorer. I said multi-agent 3/3
  against baseline 2/3. With the judge:
  - Accuracy: multi-agent 2/3 plus 1 needing review, baseline 2/3.
  - The review is multi-agent inc-016. It named the backfill job, but folded the
    pool cut into the primary cause ("when FinOps simultaneously lowered the
    pool…"). The judge counts that as co-primary: a red herring adopted and a
    must_not violated. I think that's a strict reading, since the report
    arguably treats the pool cut as compounding. Settle it in
    evals/results/openai-3/reviews.yaml either way.

⏺ Your second case separates the two systems clearly, and there's a finding
  about the baseline you should know.

  Multi-agent (gpt-5.4 gatherers, o3 reasoner and critic)

  Run: conclusive, high confidence. 1 round, 41 tool calls, 172 s, $0.54.

  - Planner: proposed 4 hypotheses: the node-pool upgrade disrupted pods (from
    the reporter); too few pods at night (from the reporter); stale backends in
    edge-lb's pool; orders-db problems. It sent out 5 tasks.
  - Change history: the new unfiltered sweep returned both org-wide changes: the
    orders-api v5.2.0 deploy at 09:30 and the node upgrade at 22:00. The deploy
    entry notes that the explicit keepAliveTimeout=75000 and
    headersTimeout=80000 settings were removed.
  - Gatherers: 36 evidence items. The deciding ones came from the traces:
    failing requests went over reused connections idle for 5.1–5.2 s, and the
    successful request on a reused connection had been idle 1.8 s. orders-api
    itself was healthy throughout: 0.05% 5xx, flat p99, low CPU.
  - Critic: accepted. It noted that the 502s continued for 6 hours after the
    upgrade finished, which rules the upgrade out, and that the 5-second idle
    pattern holds on every failing trace.
  - Root cause: v5.2.0 dropped the explicit 75 s keep-alive, so pods now close
    idle connections after about 5 s. edge-lb reuses connections for up to 60 s,
    so a request sent on a stale socket gets reset before it reaches the pod,
    and edge-lb returns a 502. It's worse at night because low traffic leaves
    connections idle longer.
  - Actions:
    a. stop edge-lb reusing connections past the backend's idle timeout;
    b. redeploy with a keep-alive longer than edge-lb's 60 s (or roll back
       v5.2.0);
    c. add a release check that compares keep-alive and load-balancer timeouts;
    d. alert on reset reasons from reused connections.
  - Refuted: the node upgrade, night-time capacity and orders-db.

  Baseline (o3 alone)

  Run: conclusive, medium confidence. 24 tool calls, 112 s, $0.23.

  - Root cause: "The worker-node OS upgrade shortened the conntrack established 
    timeout below the edge load balancer's upstream idle timeout… The upgraded 
    node image reduced nf_conntrack_tcp_timeout_established to 30 s."
  - Actions: raise the conntrack timeout on the nodes to at least 90 s; gate
    node upgrades on sysctl values; temporarily lower the load balancer's idle
    timeout to 20 s.
  - Refuted: night-time scaling and the OOM-killed pod.

  The baseline's key fact doesn't exist in your data. Nothing in the scenario
  mentions nf_conntrack_tcp_timeout_established, a 30 s value or sysctl. The
  only "conntrack" is the old PIR-2026-021, about table exhaustion on one node.
  The baseline queried a conntrack_dropped metric, got not_found, and still
  presented the 30 s value as fact. It also said the 502s "began right after the
  node upgrade", but the 502 rate was already 0.8% at 18:00, 2.1% at 20:00 and
  4.4% at 22:00, and there's a daytime 502 at 10:12 with the same reason. The
  baseline did fetch the deploy record, but never connected the removed
  keep-alive setting to the 502s.

  What the multi-agent system missed or got wrong

  - The direct proof: it never retrieved the INFO log orders-api v5.2.0 … 
    keepAliveTimeout=5000ms (09:31). Its log searches were ERROR-level or narrow
    WARN queries. It inferred the 5 s from "Node.js default" plus the 5.1–5.2 s
    idle times. The conclusion is right, but the decisive fact was inferred
    rather than observed.
  - Onset timing: it didn't use the 18:00–22:00 rise or the 10:12 daytime 502.
    Those would also have ruled out the node upgrade on timing alone.
  - Misread data quality: several gatherers flagged the metrics as covering
    "only 00:00–06:00 of the requested window". The series are sampled every 2
    hours, so nothing was missing. That added noise to the "missing data" list.

  Two general issues this exposes

  1. Our grounding check can't catch the baseline's fabrication. It confirms
     each claim cites a real tool call, and the baseline's cited calls are real.
     It doesn't check that the claim's content appears in what those calls
     returned. The judge could be asked to flag stated facts that appear in no
     tool result. That's possible now that runs save tool data.
  2. Coarse sampling read as truncated data: the gatherer prompt should tell
     agents to compare the sampling step before calling a series truncated.

❯ yeah, first see the answer key and evaluation from outer llm and then make    
  the fix                                                                       
  id: inc-009                                                                   
  metadata:                                                                     
    id: inc-009                                                                 
    title: Load balancer 502s from a keep-alive timeout mismatch that gets      
  worse at low traffic and never appears in service telemetry                   
    primary_tag: timeout_mismatch                                               
    tags: [timeout_mismatch, telemetry_blind_spot, misleading_evidence,         
  inverse_load_correlation]                                                     
    difficulty: L4                                                              
    provenance: synthetic                                                       
    inspired_by: Sporadic load balancer 502s caused by an upstream server whose 
  keep-alive timeout is shorter than the load balancer's idle timeout, often    
  introduced by a framework or runtime change that silently alters defaults     
  ground_truth:                                                                 
    root_cause:                                                                 
      statement: The 09:30 orders-api v5.2.0 rollout migrated to a new HTTP     
  server whose default keep-alive timeout is 5s, silently dropping the explicit 
  75s setting. edge-lb reuses pooled upstream connections and has a 60s idle    
  timeout, so it keeps idle connections that orders-api has already closed.     
  When edge-lb sends a request on such a stale connection (idle about 5.0 to    
  5.3s), the connection is reset before any response and edge-lb returns 502.   
  orders-api never receives these requests, so its own error rate, latency and  
  /healthz stay clean. The per-request failure rate rises as traffic falls,     
  because at low traffic pooled connections sit idle near the 5s boundary       
  instead of staying busy.                                                      
      fault_type: keepalive_timeout_mismatch                                    
      entity: orders-api HTTP server keep-alive timeout vs edge-lb upstream     
  idle timeout                                                                  
      aliases: [stale pooled connection, connection reset on connection reuse,  
  idle timeout mismatch, keep-alive race,                                       
  upstream_connection_reset_before_response]                                    
    contributing_factors:                                                       
    - statement: Telemetry blind spot. The failing requests never reach         
  orders-api, so app 5xx (0.05%), p99 latency and /healthz stay flat. The       
  signal exists only in edge-lb metrics, logs and traces.                       
      must_mention: true                                                        
    - statement: The error rate is inversely correlated with traffic (about     
  0.15% to 0.3% at peak, 7.6% at 04:00 with 110 rps), which hid the regression  
  during the day and made it look like a night-time capacity or infrastructure  
  problem.                                                                      
      must_mention: true                                                        
    - statement: The invariant that the app keep-alive must exceed the load     
  balancer idle timeout is not documented, encoded, or tested. The migration    
  checklist did not cover keep-alive, and staging has no load balancer in front 
  of the pods.                                                                  
      must_mention: true                                                        
    - statement: The 502 alert is percentage-based and only crossed             
  ──── (110 lines hidden) ───────────────────────────────────────────────────── 
  , which is why I added the deduction. Medium confidence doesn't offset it,    
  because the report stated the fabricated value without hedging.               
                                                                                
  The remediation is also harmful rather than just useless:                     
  - Raising node conntrack timeouts and gating node upgrades on sysctl values   
  leaves the real bug in place while changing production nodes.                 
  - Lowering the load balancer idle timeout to 20s does nothing, because 20s is 
  still far above the app's 5s.                                                 
                                                                                
  It earned partial credit only for refuting night-time scaling and the OOM     
  pod, and for the faint structural resemblance of its mechanism (a timeout     
  shorter than the LB idle timeout). It fetched the deploy record and never     
  connected the removed keep-alive to the 502s. The 10:12 daytime 502 with the  
  same reset reason and the 0.8% to 2.1% rise before 22:00 each disprove the    
  node-upgrade story on timing alone.                                           
                                                                                
  ## What I think the comparison shows                                          
                                                                                
  - **A stronger change sweep probably helped most:** You mention the new       
  unfiltered change sweep. Surfacing both the deploy and the upgrade removed    
  the anchoring problem that hurt both systems on inc-008. These are different  
  cases, so the score change from last time doesn't prove that, but it fits.    
  - **The multi-agent system still reasons from inference at the key moment:**  
  Level-filtered log queries missed an INFO line that was the deciding fact. A  
  gatherer rule like "always search all levels around deploy timestamps for     
  config and startup lines" would likely close that.                            
  - **The gatherers' "only 00:00 to 06:00" complaint:** I can't tell from here  
  if that is agent noise or if the tool call used a sub-window. Check the raw   
  tool output. The data has 13 points over 24 hours at a 2-hour step.           
                                                                                
  ## Fixes to my own answer key                                                 
                                                                                
  - **`ev_changes` overstates the change record:** I wrote "new server default  
  5000", but the change summary only says the explicit 75000 and 80000 were     
  removed. The 5000 appears only in the 09:31 log. Agents using just the change 
  record and traces had to infer the value, so I'd reword the key and keep      
  `ev_logs` as the decisive evidence.                                           
  - **Add an unsupported-claims check:** The key has no way to flag invented    
  specifics like the baseline's sysctl value. A field like this would help:     
                                                                                
  ```yaml                                                                       
  unsupported_claims:                                                           
  - nf_conntrack_tcp_timeout_established or any node sysctl value               
  - a "Node.js" runtime or default (the server is fastserve)                    
  ```                                                                           
                                                                                
  - **Extend `must_not`:** Add "Change node conntrack or sysctl settings" and   
  "Lower the edge-lb idle timeout to any value above 5s".

⏺ This time the baseline nailed the answer (13 calls, 50s, $0.10) using the
  keepAliveTimeout line, while the multi-agent run was inconclusive—its critic
  rightly rejected unproven assumptions but the gatherers never pulled the
  confirming log line, hitting the 60-call cap. I'm now checking the
  multi-agent's log searches to see why.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The failure was routing, not reasoning: the critic sent requests to agents
  lacking the right tools (e.g., log queries went to dependency/change-history
  agents, not observability), and the planner/critic don't know each agent's
  toolset. I'll fix this by giving the planner and critic each agent's
  capabilities so requests route correctly, and by providing the grader the
  incident report so it stops flagging supported claims as unsupported.

⏺ I've done the review, the fixes and one re-run on your case. The re-run came
  out in the baseline's favour, and it surfaced a coordination gap I've now also
  fixed.

  Grading the original runs against your key (with the updated grader, which
  sees each run's tool results):
  - Multi-agent: correct, with no red herrings or must_not violations. It
    flagged 2 claims no tool result supports: the "Node.js default", and
    "edge-lb retried the POST" (edge-lb never retries POSTs).
  - Baseline: wrong. It adopted the node-upgrade red herring and made 4
    unsupported claims, including the invented "conntrack timeout reduced to 30
    s".

  That matches the outside review's verdict. Its two factual points also check
  out:
  - Sampling complaint: "only 00:00–06:00" was agent noise; the query got every
    2-hourly point in its range.
  - Missed red herrings: the conntrack PIR was never addressed.

  What I fixed (all general, none specific to this case):
  - Grader:
    - blaming a red herring's component now counts as wrong, not partial;
    - it now lists claims supported by neither the tool results nor the incident
      report;
    - cached grades from older grading rules are re-graded.
  - Prompts, the same for both systems:
    - check a series' sampling step before calling it truncated;
    - look from before the first alert;
    - search startup and config logs at every level when a change is suspect;
    - a value is a fact only if a tool result shows it, otherwise it's labelled
      an assumption.
  - Planner: it now sees an unfiltered list of recent changes before forming
    hypotheses.
  - Routing: the planner and critic now see each specialist's toolset, and must
    send every request to an agent that can answer it.

  The re-run ($1.06; that run's code didn't yet include the routing fix):

  ┌────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────┐
  │            │           multi-agent            │        baseline        │
  ├────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Result     │ inconclusive (hit the 60-call    │ correct, no red        │
  │            │ cap)                             │ herrings               │
  ├────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Cost /     │ $0.96 / 283 s                    │ $0.10 / 50 s           │
  │ time       │                                  │                        │
  └────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────┘

  - Baseline: the startup-log guidance worked for it. It searched without a
    level filter and found keepAliveTimeout=5000ms directly.
  - Multi-agent: the planner now listed the deploy as a hypothesis, and the
    critic rightly refused the unverified "5 s default", twice. But it sent the
    right request, "get the effective server config", to the dependency and
    change-history agents, which have no log search. That's the routing gap I've
    just fixed.

  My recommendation: don't re-run this case again. We've now changed prompts
  three times based on this one scenario, and CLAUDE.md warns against tuning to
  a single case. The routing fix should be checked on tomorrow's full sweep
  instead, which will also show whether the other changes hurt elsewhere.

⏺ Grading confirms it. The new changes made the multi-agent system worse on
  Haiku, while the baseline stayed perfect:

  ┌───────────────────────────┬────────────────┬───────────────┐
  │    inc-001 to inc-008     │  multi-agent   │   baseline    │
